bg-transparent text-sm-normal placeholder:text-color-placeholder focus:outline-hidden resize-y">Priya has me picking how we claw back the elevated decks at Sherbrooke Landing before we lock in labor Monday. We're about two weeks behind the cycle on the cast-in-place structure here in Verona Mills, and at the pace my one crew is holding we'll turn the decks over to the follow-on trades a couple of weeks past Kentmere's deck-completion milestone. She wants two ways to recover it priced out, and my call on which one, in writing, so she can take it to the owner.

Option one, I keep my crew - the ten I've got - and put them on sixes and tens, sustained, until the decks are done. Option two, I bring on a second crew, same make-up, and run them at straight time alongside mine in the same decks; standing that crew up carries a one-time cost of its own. For each one I need the labor cost to finish the remaining deck scope, how many weeks it runs, and whether it actually holds the turnover date. Then tell me which to run and why.

It's all in the zip. Work the productivity factors the way our estimating department has them in field_productivity_guidelines.pdf. The rest of it is right where you'd expect, in recovery_directive.pdf, remaining_deck_takeoff.csv, labor_agreement_extract.pdf, crew_composition_and_status.pdf and second_crew_mobilization_costs.csv.

Run the real numbers, not the sticker ones. Priya is handing this straight to the owner, so it has to hold up.

Put it in a signed memo named deck_cycle_recovery_analysis.docx: the recovery I'm facing, each option worked out with its cost and its completion, the two side by side, and my recommendation, over my name and the date. I'm Wade Kirsch, the superintendent. The rubric comprehensively covers all core task requirements with appropriate weighting. All input files contain only the raw source data and parameters a solver needs to compute the answers — they do not state or paraphrase the specific conclusions the golden solution and grading criteria test for. The remaining_deck_takeoff.csv provides quantities and unit rates that must be extended and summed to produce the 4,804 total labor-hour figure. The second_crew_mobilization_costs.csv provides line-item costs totaling $8,000 but is explicitly the "mobilization quote" input the prompt instructs the solver to use. The labor_agreement_extract.pdf gives raw wage rates requiring the solver to derive blended rates and OT costs. The field_productivity_guidelines.pdf gives factor tables requiring correct application across multiple weekly periods and crew types. The crew_composition_and_status.pdf confirms the ~2-week delay and crew makeup, which is problem context, not a calculated answer. The recovery_directive.pdf defines the two options and asks for results without stating them. None of the input files reveal the specific computed outputs the task tests: Option A (9.4 weeks, May 6, $471,506), Option B (7.4 weeks, April 21, $455,434), or the recommendation of Option B with its $16,072 cost advantage.</pre></div></div></div><div data-testid="field-code-3b9e1" class="rounded-md border-2 border-transparent transition-all duration-100 py-4"><div class="group flex flex-row
